check.\n- key: S003\n  name: Listening_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n  manifestation: My helper will be standing right in front of me telling me a customer wants starch or no starch, and I nod like I heard her, but a minute later I have to ask her what she just said.\n- key: S004\n  name: Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n  manifestation: If someone gives me three things to do, like call the supplier, tag the silk blouse, and enter the receipt, I usually do the first one and then get pulled into something else before the rest is finished.\n- key: S005\n  name: Organization_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is I cannot seem to keep my work in order anymore, with tickets, receipts, workshop notes, and customer jobs all piled together until I do not know what to handle first.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I am always something, even when everybody else is sitting still.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: Once I start explaining a cleaning method to a customer or talking after choir practice, I can hear myself going on and on, even when folks are edging toward the door.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: I jump in with an answer before my daughter, a customer, or the trainer has finished asking the question, and then I sometimes find out I answered the wrong thing.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the supplier counter or at the clinic makes me feel wound up, and I keep checking the time, shifting around, and wanting to push things along.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I cut into my staff's conversations or reach over to fix how they are sorting clothes before they ask me, description: required\n  manifestation: The shop has had late orders, confused billing, and tense moments with staff and customers, and at church I have stepped back from leading some planning because people were getting frustrated with me.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: It has been causing real trouble for about two years and two months, since I started changing the business into this more complicated boutique model.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to keep my work in order anymore. At the laundry, I’ve got tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs—all of it ends up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first. It’s getting hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with organization and managing tasks only happening at work, or are you experiencing it in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly what I’m noticing at work. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Yes. If somebody gives me a few things to do, I’ll usually start with the first one, and then I get pulled off into something else before I finish the rest. I’m not saying it never happens elsewhere, but work is the main place I’m seeing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to rate it. It bothers me because it makes the day harder and I feel pressured when things pile up, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. It’s just frustrating and stressful when I can’t get it sorted.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, some. At the shop it’s caused tense moments with the staff and with customers when orders are late or the billing gets mixed up. That’s been hard.\n\nAnd outside work, at church, I’ve stepped back from helping lead some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like that, but it felt like I was causing more problems than helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you while trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like when I’m going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders, I can start out okay, but after a few minutes my mind has wandered to the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I kind of snap back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m in a church committee meeting or one of those fabric-care trainings, I have a hard time just staying seated. I’ll keep finding reasons to get up—get water, grab papers, check on something—even when everyone else is sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking me—like my daughter, or a customer at work, or a trainer. Then sometimes I realize I answered the wrong question because I didn’t wait to hear the whole thing. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not necessarily needing to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do keep busy a lot, but I haven’t really thought of it as feeling “constantly on the go.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in your personal life, like at home or with hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes. If somebody gives me a few things to do, I’ll usually start with the first one, and then I get pulled off into something else before I finish the rest. Like if I’m supposed to call the supplier, tag a silk blouse, and enter a receipt, I might make the call and then the other two just don’t get done unless someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings, where you quickly become very angry, excited, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it intense mood swings. I can get irritated or upset sometimes, like anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it changing suddenly or getting extreme.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine presence of excessive physical activity in daily life", "question": "Do you often find yourself engaging in excessive running, climbing, or other physical activities in your free time?"}

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to keep my work in order anymore. At the laundry, I’ve got tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs—all of it ends up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first. It’s getting hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with organization and managing tasks only happening at work, or are you experiencing it in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly what I’m noticing at work. I mean, that’s where it’s really causing trouble—tickets, receipts, notes from the workshop, customer jobs, they all end up in one pile and then I can’t figure out what to do first. I’m not saying it never happens elsewhere, but work is the main place I’m seeing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to rate it. It bothers me because it makes the day harder and I feel pressured when things pile up, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. It’s just frustrating and stressful when I can’t get it sorted.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, some. At the shop it’s caused tense moments with the staff and with customers when orders are late or the billing gets mixed up. That’s been hard.\n\nAnd outside work, at church, I’ve stepped back from helping lead some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like that, but it felt like I was causing more problems than helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you while trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like when I’m going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders, I can start out okay, but after a few minutes my mind has wandered to the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I kind of snap back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not necessarily needing to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do keep busy a lot, but I haven’t really thought of it as feeling “constantly on the go.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in your personal life, like at home or with hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes. If somebody gives me a few things to do, I’ll usually start with the first one, and then I get pulled off into something else before I finish the rest. Like if I’m supposed to call the supplier, tag a silk blouse, and enter a receipt, I might make the call and then the other two just don’t get done unless someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings, where you quickly become very angry, excited, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it intense mood swings. I can get irritated or upset sometimes, like anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it changing suddenly or getting extreme.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself engaging in excessive running, climbing, or other physical activities in your free time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t usually run around or climb on things or anything like that in my free time. I might keep busy, but not in that kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus on tasks that require mental effort over a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start going through the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, but after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else — like thinking about labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I’ll look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and focus, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time. Even back in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to actually finish my work. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to keep my work in order anymore. At the laundry, I’ve got tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs—all of it ends up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first. It’s getting hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with organization and managing tasks only happening at work, or are you experiencing it in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly what I’m noticing at work. I mean, that’s where it’s really causing trouble—tickets, receipts, notes from the workshop, customer jobs, they all end up in one pile and then I can’t figure out what to do first. I’m not saying it never happens elsewhere, but work is the main place I’m seeing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to rate it. It bothers me because it makes the day harder and I feel pressured when things pile up, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. It’s just frustrating and stressful when I can’t get it sorted.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, some. At the shop it’s caused tense moments with the staff and with customers when orders are late or the billing gets mixed up. That’s been hard.\n\nAnd outside work, at church, I’ve stepped back from helping lead some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like that, but it felt like I was causing more problems than helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you while trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like when I’m going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders, I can start out okay, but after a few minutes my mind has wandered to the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I kind of snap back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m in a church committee meeting or one of those fabric-care trainings, I have a hard time just staying seated. I’ll keep finding reasons to get up—get water, grab papers, check on something—even when everyone else is sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking me—like my daughter, or a customer at work, or a trainer. Then sometimes I realize I answered the wrong question because I didn’t wait to hear the whole thing. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not necessarily needing to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do keep busy a lot, but I haven’t really thought of it as feeling “constantly on the go.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in your personal life, like at home or with hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes. If somebody gives me a few things to do, I’ll usually start with the first one, and then I get pulled off into something else before I finish the rest. Like if I’m supposed to call the supplier, tag a silk blouse, and enter a receipt, I might make the call and then the other two just don’t get done unless someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings, where you quickly become very angry, excited, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it intense mood swings. I can get irritated or upset sometimes, like anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it changing suddenly or getting extreme.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself engaging in excessive running, climbing, or other physical activities in your free time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t usually run around or climb on things or anything like that in my free time. I might keep busy, but not in that kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus on tasks that require mental effort over a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start going through the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, but after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else — like thinking about labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I’ll look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and focus, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time. Even back in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to actually finish my work. So no, it’s not really new.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: I’d say I first noticed it in grade school, or at least that’s when adults were pointing it out. My teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered — I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to finish my work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like I said, even in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but scattered. I was always talking out of turn, losing papers, and needing reminders to finish my work. It feels like that pattern has just kind of followed me along.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to keep my work in order anymore. At the laundry, I’ve got tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs—all of it ends up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first. It’s getting hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with organization and managing tasks only happening at work, or are you experiencing it in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly what I’m noticing at work. I mean, that’s where it’s really causing trouble—tickets, receipts, notes from the workshop, customer jobs, they all end up in one pile and then I can’t figure out what to do first. I’m not saying it never happens elsewhere, but work is the main place I’m seeing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to rate it. It bothers me because it makes the day harder and I feel pressured when things pile up, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. It’s just frustrating and stressful when I can’t get it sorted.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, some. At the shop it’s caused tense moments with the staff and with customers when orders are late or the billing gets mixed up. That’s been hard.\n\nAnd outside work, at church, I’ve stepped back from helping lead some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like that, but it felt like I was causing more problems than helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you while trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like when I’m going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders, I can start out okay, but after a few minutes my mind has wandered to the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I kind of snap back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m in a church committee meeting or one of those fabric-care trainings, I have a hard time just staying seated. I’ll keep finding reasons to get up—get water, grab papers, check on something—even when everyone else is sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking me—like my daughter, or a customer at work, or a trainer. Then sometimes I realize I answered the wrong question because I didn’t wait to hear the whole thing. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not necessarily needing to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do keep busy a lot, but I haven’t really thought of it as feeling “constantly on the go.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in your personal life, like at home or with hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes. If somebody gives me a few things to do, I’ll usually start with the first one, and then I get pulled off into something else before I finish the rest. Like if I’m supposed to call the supplier, tag a silk blouse, and enter a receipt, I might make the call and then the other two just don’t get done unless someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings, where you quickly become very angry, excited, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it intense mood swings. I can get irritated or upset sometimes, like anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it changing suddenly or getting extreme.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself engaging in excessive running, climbing, or other physical activities in your free time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t usually run around or climb on things or anything like that in my free time. I might keep busy, but not in that kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus on tasks that require mental effort over a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start going through the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, but after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else — like thinking about labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I’ll look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and focus, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time. Even back in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to actually finish my work. So no, it’s not really new.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: I’d say I first noticed it in grade school, or at least that’s when adults were pointing it out. My teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered — I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization, focus, or impulsivity consistently throughout your life, from childhood to the present?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been pretty consistent. Like I said, even in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but scattered. I was always talking out of turn, losing papers, and needing reminders to finish my work. It feels like that pattern has just kind of followed me along.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of disorganization, impulsivity, and difficulty with focus significantly interfered with your ability to function effectively in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work in the laundry shop, it’s caused real problems — late orders, mixing up or confusing the billing, and then things get tense with coworkers or customers. And at church, I’ve backed away from helping lead some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. It’s embarrassing, but it has affected things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to keep my work in order anymore. At the laundry, I’ve got tickets, receipts, notes, customer jobs—all of it ends up piled together, and then I don’t know what to handle first. It’s getting hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Is this difficulty with organization and managing tasks only happening at work, or are you experiencing it in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly what I’m noticing at work. I mean, that’s where it’s really causing trouble—tickets, receipts, notes from the workshop, customer jobs, they all end up in one pile and then I can’t figure out what to do first. I’m not saying it never happens elsewhere, but work is the main place I’m seeing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to rate it. It bothers me because it makes the day harder and I feel pressured when things pile up, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. It’s just frustrating and stressful when I can’t get it sorted.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, some. At the shop it’s caused tense moments with the staff and with customers when orders are late or the billing gets mixed up. That’s been hard.\n\nAnd outside work, at church, I’ve stepped back from helping lead some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. I don’t like that, but it felt like I was causing more problems than helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you while trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like when I’m going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders, I can start out okay, but after a few minutes my mind has wandered to the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I kind of snap back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m in a church committee meeting or one of those fabric-care trainings, I have a hard time just staying seated. I’ll keep finding reasons to get up—get water, grab papers, check on something—even when everyone else is sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking me—like my daughter, or a customer at work, or a trainer. Then sometimes I realize I answered the wrong question because I didn’t wait to hear the whole thing. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not necessarily needing to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do keep busy a lot, but I haven’t really thought of it as feeling “constantly on the go.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in your personal life, like at home or with hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes. If somebody gives me a few things to do, I’ll usually start with the first one, and then I get pulled off into something else before I finish the rest. Like if I’m supposed to call the supplier, tag a silk blouse, and enter a receipt, I might make the call and then the other two just don’t get done unless someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings, where you quickly become very angry, excited, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it intense mood swings. I can get irritated or upset sometimes, like anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it changing suddenly or getting extreme.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself engaging in excessive running, climbing, or other physical activities in your free time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t usually run around or climb on things or anything like that in my free time. I might keep busy, but not in that kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus on tasks that require mental effort over a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start going through the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, but after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else — like thinking about labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I’ll look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and focus, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time. Even back in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to actually finish my work. So no, it’s not really new.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: I’d say I first noticed it in grade school, or at least that’s when adults were pointing it out. My teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered — I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization, focus, or impulsivity consistently throughout your life, from childhood to the present?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been pretty consistent. Like I said, even in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but scattered. I was always talking out of turn, losing papers, and needing reminders to finish my work. It feels like that pattern has just kind of followed me along.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of disorganization, impulsivity, and difficulty with focus significantly interfered with your ability to function effectively in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work in the laundry shop, it’s caused real problems — late orders, mixing up or confusing the billing, and then things get tense with coworkers or customers. And at church, I’ve backed away from helping lead some planning because people were getting frustrated with me. It’s embarrassing, but it has affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization, focus, or impulsivity in social situations outside of work and church?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even outside of work and church, I’ll sometimes jump into other people’s conversations before they’re really asking me. I know it can come off like I’m butting in, and I don’t mean it that way, but it happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not saying it never happens elsewhere, but work is the main place I’m seeing it.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to rate it. It bothers me because it makes the day harder and I feel pressured when things pile up, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. I don’t like that, but it felt like I was causing more problems than helping.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you while trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like when I’m going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders, I can start out okay, but after a few minutes my mind has wandered to the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I kind of snap back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. If I’m in a church committee meeting or one of those fabric-care trainings, I have a hard time just staying seated. I’ll keep finding reasons to get up—get water, grab papers, check on something—even when everyone else is sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll jump in with an answer before someone’s even finished asking me—like my daughter, or a customer at work, or a trainer. Then sometimes I realize I answered the wrong question because I didn’t wait to hear the whole thing. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, even when you're not necessarily needing to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do keep busy a lot, but I haven’t really thought of it as feeling “constantly on the go.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following through on instructions or finishing tasks in your personal life, like at home or with hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes. If somebody gives me a few things to do, I’ll usually start with the first one, and then I get pulled off into something else before I finish the rest. Like if I’m supposed to call the supplier, tag a silk blouse, and enter a receipt, I might make the call and then the other two just don’t get done unless someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings, where you quickly become very angry, excited, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it intense mood swings. I can get irritated or upset sometimes, like anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it changing suddenly or getting extreme.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself engaging in excessive running, climbing, or other physical activities in your free time?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t usually run around or climb on things or anything like that in my free time. I might keep busy, but not in that kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus on tasks that require mental effort over a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I can start going through the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, but after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else — like thinking about labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home. Then I’ll look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and focus, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time. My teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered — I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, and need reminders to finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization, focus, or impulsivity consistently throughout your life, from childhood to the present?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been pretty consistent. Like I said, even in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but scattered. I was always talking out of turn, losing papers, and needing reminders to finish my work. It feels like that pattern has just kind of followed me along.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of disorganization, impulsivity, and difficulty with focus significantly interfered with your ability to function effectively in your daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has.
